We construct now a doubly minimal  $\mathbb{Z}^2$-subshift. We first need to introduce some notions. \par 
A {\it block} will be a coloration of some rectangle, i.e. an element of  $A^{[0,  h)\times [0,h')}$ for some $h,h'> 0$. Let ${\bf e}_1$, ${\bf e}_2$ denote the canonical base. A {\it concatenation} of two blocks ${\bf H} \in A^{[0,  h_1)\times [0,h)} $ and ${\bf K} \in  A^{[0,  k_1)\times [0,h)}$ {\it in the direction} ${\bf e}_1$ is the block denoted ${\bf H} {\bf K} \in A^{[0,  h_1+k_1)\times [0,h)}$ defined as 
\[ {\bf H}{\bf K}|_{[0,  h_1)\times [0,h) } = {\bf H} \textrm{ and } {\bf H}{\bf K} ( j ) = {\bf K}(j-h_1 {\bf e}_1) \textrm{ for } j \in {[h_1,  h_1+k_1)\times [0,h) }.
\]
One defines in a similar way the concatenation of blocks in the direction ${\bf e}_2$.  

In the same way as in the one dimensional case, we construct inductively blocks on $\mathbb{Z}^2$ in two steps. We will concatenate block in the ${\bf e}_1$ direction according to the order in \eqref{eq:Oprocha}. Then we will concatenate the obtained blocks in the ${\bf e}_2$ direction according to the same order.  
Assuming ${\bf H}_{n-1}^{(0)}$ and ${\bf H}_{n-1}^{(1)}$ are blocks of $A^{[0,h_{n-1})^2}$, then  ${\bf H}_{n}^{(0, {\bf e}_1)}$ and ${\bf H}_{n}^{(1, {\bf e}_1)}$  will be concatenations of  blocks in the direction ${\bf e}_1$, in the same order as in \eqref{eq:Oprocha}, that is
\begin{align*}\label{eq:Oprochae1}
       {\bf H}_n^{(0, {\bf e}_1)} &= ({\bf H}_{n-1}^{(0)})^{k_n} {\bf U}_{n-1}^{(3, {\bf e}_1)}({\bf H}_{n-1}^{(1)}\mathtt{a}_{n-1}^{{\bf e}_1})^{k_n}{\bf U}_{n-1}^{(4, {\bf e}_1)}({\bf H}_{n-1}^{(0)})^{k_n}{\bf U}_{n-1}^{(5, {\bf e}_1)}({\bf H}_{n-1}^{(1)}\mathtt{a}_{n-1}^{{\bf e}_1})^{k_n}{\bf H}_{n-1}^{(0)} \\
       {\bf H}_n^{(1, {\bf e}_1)} &= ({\bf H}_{n-1}^{(0)}{\bf H}_{n-1}^{(1)}\mathtt{a}_{n-1}^{{\bf e}_1})^{k_n/2}{\bf U}_{n-1}^{(3, {\bf e}_1)}({\bf H}_{n-1}^{(0)}{\bf H}_{n-1}^{(1)}\mathtt{a}_{n-1}^{{\bf e}_1})^{k_n/2}{\bf U}_{n-1}^{(4, {\bf e}_1)}({\bf H}_{n-1}^{(0)}{\bf H}_{n-1}^{(1)}\mathtt{a}_{n-1}^{{\bf e}_1})^{k_n/2} \addtocounter{equation}{1}\tag{\theequation}\\
        & \hspace{8cm}{\bf U}_{n-1}^{(5, {\bf e}_1)}({\bf H}_{n-1}^{(0)}{\bf H}_{n-1}^{(1)}\mathtt{a}_{n-1}^{{\bf e}_1})^{k_n/2}{\bf H}_{n-1}^{(1)},
   \end{align*}
where $k_n$ is an even number such that $k_n > h_{n-1}(2  h_{n-1} +1)$; 
$\mathtt{a}_{n-1}^{{\bf e}_1}\in A^{\{0\} \times [0, h_{n-1})}$ denotes the coloration with the single color $\mathtt{a}$   and  ${\bf U}_{n-1}^{(k, {\bf e}_1)}= {\bf H}_{n-1}^{(1)}\mathtt{a}_{n-1}^{{\bf e}_1}({\bf H}_{n-1}^{(0)})^k{\bf H}_{n-1}^{(1)}\mathtt{a}_{n-1}^{{\bf e}_1}$ for $k=3,4, 5$.
It is worth noting, from the properties of $H_n^{(0)}, H_n^{(1)}$ that ${\bf H}_n^{(0,{\bf e}_1)}$ and ${\bf H}_n^{(1,{\bf e}_1)}$ are colorations of the same rectangle $[0, h_n) \times [0, h_{n-1})$ and each block  ${\bf H}_n^{(0, {\bf e}_1)}$ and ${\bf H}_n^{(1, {\bf e}_1)}\mathtt{a}_{n-1}^{{\bf e}_1}$ are concatenations in the direction ${\bf e}_1$ of blocks ${\bf H}_{n-1}^{(0)}$ and  ${\bf H}_{n-1}^{(1)}\mathtt{a}_{n-1}^{{\bf e}_1}$,  ${\bf H}_n^{(0, {\bf e}_1)}$ is always followed by   ${\bf H}_n^{(0, {\bf e}_1)}$ or  ${\bf H}_n^{(1, {\bf e}_1)}$.

\begin{figure}[ht!]
\centering
    \includestandalone[width = \textwidth]{fig/oprocha_horizontal}
    \label{fig:op_hor}
    \caption{For the first step, the square blocks of the previous iteration, namely ${\bf H}_{n-1}^{(0)}$ and ${\bf H}_{n-1}^{(1)}$, are arranged following equation~\ref{eq:Oprochae1} where the blocks $\mathtt{a}^{e_1}_{n-1}$ are represented as red blocks.}
\end{figure}
                                                               
In a second step, we proceed similarly in the direction ${\bf e}_2$ but starting from the blocks ${\bf H}_n^{(0,{\bf e}_1)}, {\bf H}_n^{(1,{\bf e}_1)}$ instead of  ${\bf H}_{n-1}^{(0)}, {\bf H}_{n-1}^{(1)}$ and by concatenating them in the ${\bf e}_2$ direction, still in the same order as \eqref{eq:Oprocha}. Formally, the   blocks ${\bf H}_n^{(0,{\bf e}_2)}$ and ${\bf H}_n^{(1,{\bf e}_2)}$ are given by formulas \eqref{eq:Oprochae1} with  ${\bf H}_n^{(i,{\bf e}_1)}$ instead of ${\bf H}_{n-1}^{(i)}$, $i=0,1$, 
${\bf e}_2$ instead of ${\bf e}_1$ and where $\mathtt{a}_{n-1}^{{\bf e}_2}\in A^{[0, h_{n}) \times \{0\}}$ denotes the coloration with the single color $\mathtt{a}$. Thanks to the properties of $H_n^{(j)}$, $j=0,1$, it is direct to check that the blocks ${\bf H}_n^{(i,{\bf e}_2)}$, $i= 0,1$ are colorations of the square $[0, h_n)^2$. Moreover ${\bf H}_n^{(0,{\bf e}_2)}$,  ${\bf H}_n^{(1, {\bf e}_2)}\mathtt{a}_{n-1}^{{\bf e}_2}$ are concatenations in the direction ${\bf e}_2$ of blocks ${\bf H}_{n-1}^{(0, {\bf e}_2)}$ and  ${\bf H}_{n-1}^{(1, {\bf e}_2)}\mathtt{a}_{n-1}^{{\bf e}_2}$. The block ${\bf H}_{n}^{(0, {\bf e}_2)}$ is always followed, in the direction ${\bf e}_2$, by  ${\bf H}_{n}^{(0, {\bf e}_2)}$ or  ${\bf H}_{n}^{(1, {\bf e}_2)}$. 

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
    \includestandalone[width = \textwidth]{fig/oprocha_vertical}
    \label{fig:op_ver}
    \caption{For the second step, we obtain new square blocks by concatenating the rectangles ${\bf H}_{n}^{(i,e_1)}$ following an analog of equation~\ref{eq:Oprocha}, where once again the blocks $\mathtt{a}^{e_2}_{n-1}$ are represented as red blocks.}
\end{figure}
\noindent Finally, we set ${\bf H}_n^{(0)} = {\bf H}_n^{(0, {\bf e}_2)}$ and  ${\bf H}_n^{(1)} = {\bf H}_n^{(1, {\bf e}_2)}$.

Since the blocks are obtained by concatenating blocks in the same order as in the one dimensional case (see Equation \eqref{eq:Oprocha}), we get similar combinatorial properties as in Lemma \ref{lem:Oprocha}.   
\begin{lemma}\label{lem:Oprocha2D}
Let  $n \ge t, v >2$ be  integers and  $k= (k_{1}, k_{2}) \in \mathbb{Z}^2$  be a vector.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
	\item     If  $\|k\|_\infty = \max(|k_{1}|, |k_{2}|)  \le h_{n-1}/2$, then the blocks ${\bf H}_n^{(\alpha)}$ and  $T^{-k}{\bf H}_n^{(1-\alpha)}$, $\alpha= 0,1$, have a coincidence outside the ball of radius $\|k\|_\infty$. More precisely, there  is a position $i \in \mathbb{Z}^2$, $\|k\|_\infty \le \|i\|_\infty \le h_n-h_{n-1} $  such that
     ${\bf H}_n^{(\alpha)}|_{i+ [0,h_{n-1})^2} = {\bf H}_n^{(1-\alpha)}|_{i+k +[0,h_{n-1})^2} = {\bf H}_{n-1}^{(0)}$.

  \item If $h_{t-1}/2 <k_{1} \le  h_{t}/2$  and  $h_{v-1}/2 <k_{2} \le  h_{v}/2$, with $t,v \le n-2$ then the blocks ${\bf H}_{n}^{\alpha}$ and $T^{-k} {\bf H}_{n}^{\beta}$, $\alpha, \beta \in \{0,1\}$ have a coincidence. More precisely, denoting $u= \max (t, v)$, there is a position $q\in \mathbb{Z}^{2}$ such that $q+ [0, h_{u})^{2}$ and   $q+k+ [0, h_{u})^{2}$ are subset of $[0, h_{n})^{2}$ and 
  ${\bf H}_{n}^{(\alpha)}|_{q+ [0, h_{u})^{2}}=     {\bf H}_{n}^{(\beta)}|_{q+k+ [0, h_{u})^{2}} = {\bf H}_{{u}}^{(0)}$.
\end{enumerate}    
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof} Since the blocks are concatenations in the ${\bf e}_2$ direction of  blocks ${\bf H}_n^{(0, {\bf e}_1)}$ and ${\bf H}_n^{(1, {\bf e}_1)}\mathtt{a}^{{\bf e}_2}_{n-1}$ ordered as the words $H_n^{(0)}$, $H_n^{(1)}$, by Lemma \ref{lem:Oprocha} (i), there is an integer $i'_2 \ge |k_2|$ such that ${\bf H}_n^{(\alpha)}|_{(0,i'_2) + [0,h_{n}) \times [0,h_{n-1})} = {\bf H}_n^{(1-\alpha)}|_{(k_1,k_2+i'_2) + [0,h_{n}) \times [0,h_{n-1})} = {\bf H}_n^{(0, {\bf e}_1)}$. 
Notice that this level do not correspond to the top row of the block: precisely $i'_2 + k_2 +h_{n-1} < k_2 + h_n$, because the condition $\|k\|_\infty  \le h_{n-1}/2$ would impose  on the top rows of ${\bf H}_n^{(0)} $ and ${\bf H}_n^{(1)} $ to be equal. 


\noindent Since the block ${\bf H}_n^{(0, {\bf e}_1)}$ is always followed, in the ${\bf e}_2$ direction, by ${\bf H}_n^{(0, {\bf e}_1)}$ or  ${\bf H}_n^{(1, {\bf e}_1)}$,   
there is an integer  $i_2 \ge i'_2$ such that ${\bf H}_n^{(\alpha)}|_{(0,i_2) + [0,h_{n}) \times [0,h_{n-1})} ={\bf H}_n^{(\beta, {\bf e}_1)}$  and  \[{\bf H}_n^{(1-\alpha)}|_{(k_1,i_2+k_2) + [0,h_{n}) \times [0,h_{n-1})} = {\bf H}_n^{(1-\beta, {\bf e}_1)},\] for some $\beta \in \{0,1\}$.
Again,  Lemma \ref{lem:Oprocha} (i) applied to ${\bf H}_n^{(\beta, {\bf e}_1)}$ and $T^{-(k_{1},0)}{\bf H}_{n}^{(1-\beta, {\bf e}_{1})}$, gives the existence of an integer $i_1 \ge |k_1|$ such that $i =(i_1, i_2)$ satisfies the statement of Item (\textit{i}).


To prove Item (\textit{ii}), let us consider the case where  $v \ge t$. The other case is similar. The situation $\alpha\neq \beta$ is treated in item (\textit{i}), so we assume $\alpha=\beta$.
Recall that the left bottom block of ${\bf H}_{n}^{(\alpha)}$ is of the form ${\bf H}_{v+1}^{(0)}$. 
Since this block is always followed, in the ${\bf e}_1$ direction, by ${\bf H}_{v+1}^{(0)}$ or  ${\bf H}_{v+1}^{(1)}$,   
there is an integer  $i_2$ such that ${\bf H}_n^{(\alpha)}|_{(0,i_2h_{v+1}) + [0,h_{v+1})^{2}} ={\bf H}_{v+1}^{(1)}$  and  ${\bf H}_{n}^{(\alpha)}|_{(0,(i_{2}-1)h_{v+1}) + [0,h_{v+1})^{2}} = {\bf H}_{v+1}^{(0)}$.
By Item (\textit{i}), the blocks  $T^{-k} {\bf H}_{v+1}^{(0)}$ and ${\bf H}_{v+1}^{(1)}$ have a coincidence.  
\end{proof}

Let $x\in \{\mathtt{0},\mathtt{1},\mathtt{a} \}^{\mathbb{Z}^2}$ be a point  such that for each $n>0$ $x|_{[-h_n, h_n)^2} (j)   = {\bf H}_{n+1}^{(0)}|_{[0, 2h_n)^2} ( j+ h_n {\bf e}_1 +h_n {\bf e}_2)$, for $ j \in [-h_n, h_n)^2$.  This means that $ x|_{[-h_n, h_n)^2}$ is the double concatenation in the direction ${\bf e}_2$ of the   double concatenation  in the direction ${\bf e}_1$ of ${\bf H}_n^{(0)}$. This block is actually a sub-block of the one defined at the level $n+1$ so that the blocks appearing in $x$ are the same as the ones appearing in ${\bf H}_n^{(\alpha)}$, $\alpha =0,1$.

\begin{lemma}
The $\mathbb{Z}^2$-subshift $X= \overline{\textrm{orb}_T (x)}$ that is the orbit closure of $x$ is minimal and aperiodic, i.e the stabilizer of any point is trivial. 
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The block  $x|_{[-h_n, h_n)^2}$ appears in the left bottom of each block ${\bf H}_{n+1}^{(0)}$ which appears in each block  ${\bf H}_m^{(0)}$ and  ${\bf H}_m^{(1)}$, $m>n+1$. Hence $x|_{[-h_n, h_n)^2}$ occurs syndetically. The  orbit closure  $X$  for the shift map $T$ of $x$ is then a minimal subshift. 

The subshift being minimal, any potential period $v= (v_{1}, v_{2})\in \mathbb{Z}^2\setminus\{\bf 0\}$ is a period for $x$: $T^v x = x$.  Take $n$ large enough so that $h_{n-2}/2>\|v\|_\infty$. In particular this means that for each  integers $k\in \mathbb{Z}$, $m\in \mathbb{N}^{*}$, the patterns $T^{kv}\mathtt{a}_{m}^{{\bf e}_{1}}$ and $T^{kv}\mathtt{a}_{m}^{{\bf e}_{2}}$ occur in $x$. This imposes that there is a translated of a sublattice on which the restriction of $x$ is $\mathtt{a}$: there is a position $q \in \mathbb{Z}^{2}$ such that for any $(\ell^{1}, \ell^{2}) \in \mathbb{Z}^{2}$, $x_{q+(v_{1}\ell^{1}, v_{2} \ell^{2}) } = \mathtt{a}$.   So, this is also the case for the pattern ${\bf H}_{m}^{(0)}$. Since the patterns   ${\bf H}_{m}^{(0)}$ and $T^{-{\bf k}e_{i}} {\bf H}_{m}^{(0)}$, for  any  $m> n+2$, $ i= 1,2$ and $0 \le k \le \| v \|_{\infty}$, coincide on a pattern ${\bf H}_{n}^{(0)}$ (Lemma \ref{lem:Oprocha2D}), the pattern ${\bf H}_{n}^{(0)}$ is covered by all the translations of the lattice $(v_{1},0) \mathbb{Z} \oplus (0,v_{2})\mathbb{Z}$ where the coloration is constant to $\mathtt{a}$. Hence the coloration $x$ is constant, a contradiction.  
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
The system  $(X, T, \mathbb{Z}^{2})$ is doubly minimal.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
To prove double minimality, it suffices to show that any pair of points $y,z$  in different orbits are proximal, i.e. the  distance   between $T^n y $  and $T^n z$ goes to zero for an infinite sequence of $n\in \mathbb{Z}^2$ \cite[p.747]{King_top_minimal_self_joinings:1990}.

Indeed, since $y$ is decomposed into blocks of the form
${\bf H}_n^{(0)} $ and ${\bf H}_n^{(1)}$,  
for each integer $n>0$, there are integers $ - h_n/2 \le i_1(n), i_2(n) \le h_n/2$  such that $y_{(i_1,i_2)+ [0, h_n)^2} = {\bf H}_n^{(\alpha_{n}, {\bf e}_2)}$, for some $\alpha_{n} \in \{0, 1\}$. Similarly, there are integers $ i_\ell - h_{n-2}/2 \le j_\ell(n) \le i_\ell+h_{n-2}/2$, $\ell= 1,2$ such that $z_{(j_1,j_2)+ [0, h_{n-2})^2} = {\bf H}_{n-2}^{(\beta_{n}, {\bf e}_2)}$  with  $\beta_{n} \in \{ 0,1\}$.  In particular, the indices $i_\ell$ and $j_\ell$ are close in the following sense $|i_\ell-j_\ell| \le h_{n-2}/2$. 
Let $v_n$ and $t_n$ be the smallest integers such that  $(h_{v_{n}-1}/2 <)$ $|i_2-j_2| \le h_{v_n}/2$ and  $(h_{t_{n}-1}/2 <)$ $|i_1-j_1| \le h_{t_n}/2$.

Setting $u_{n} = \max (v_{n}, t_{n})$, Lemma \ref{lem:Oprocha2D} (\textit{ii}) provides the existence of one  position $q_{n} \in \mathbb{Z}^{2}$ such that
\begin{align}\label{eq:Oprocha2}
y|_{q_{n}+ [0, h_{u_n})^{2} } = z|_{q_{n}+ [0, h_{u_n})^{2}} = {\bf H}_{u_{n}}^{(0)}.
\end{align}
In particular,  the elements $y$ and $z$ have coincidence.

Doing the former step for each integer $n$ provides integer sequences $(v_n)_{n\ge 0}$ and $(t_n)_{n \ge 0}$.  If  one of the two sequences is unbounded, the elements $y$ and $z$ coincide  on larger and larger set (of size the same size as ${\bf H}_{\max (v_n, t_n)}^{(0)}$ by equation \eqref{eq:Oprocha2}). Hence the two elements are proximal.

In contrast, let us consider the case where the two sequences $(v_n)_n$ and $(t_n)_n$ are bounded, Then, the positions $i(n) = (i_{1}, i_{2})$ and $j(n)= (j_{1}, j_{2})$ belong  to a bounded set. So, up to taking a subsequence of $n$, we can assume that the positions $i$ and $j$ are independent of $n$. 
We claim that the points $y$ and $z$ are proximal or either in the same orbit. To prove it, let us assume they are not proximal. This means they can not coincide on sufficiently large ball. So, there is some integer $N_1 >0$ such that $y$ and $z$ cannot coincide on any square of size $h_n$ for $n >N_1$. Lemma \ref{lem:Oprocha2D} (\textit{i}) then ensures that $y|_{i+[0, h_n)^2} = z|_{j+[0, h_n)^2} = {\bf H}_n^{(\alpha_n)}$ for the same $\alpha_n \in \{0,1\}$ for any $n>N_1+1$. Also, the same lemma gives that $y|_{i+ (\epsilon_1  h_n, \epsilon_2  h_n)+[0, h_n)^2} = z|_{j+(\epsilon_1  h_n, \epsilon_2  h_n)+[0, h_n)^2}$ for any $\epsilon_1, \epsilon_2 \in \{-1, 0, 1\}$. Since it is true for infinitely many $n$, this provides that $T^{-i}y= T^{-j}z$ meaning that $y$ and $z$ are in the same orbit. 
\end{proof}

The construction of a $\mathbb{Z}^d$ subshift that is doubly minimal follows the same strategy as in dimension $2$. By concatenating blocks iteratively in each direction ${\bf e}_{2}, \ldots, {\bf e}_{d}$ according to the same order as \eqref{eq:Oprocha}.  The properties and the proofs are the same.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
{{\cite[Lemma 4.5]{Oprocha_double_minimal:2019}}}\label{lem:Oprocha} 
Let $\mathtt{b}$ be an auxiliary symbol $\mathtt{b} \notin \{\mathtt{0},\mathtt{1},\mathtt{a}\}$ and let $k\in \mathbb{N}^*$ be an integer.  

If $ k \le h_{n}/2$,  then for any  $\alpha \in \{0,1\}$, the words  $u= H_{n}^{(\alpha)}\mathtt{b}^k$
 and $w=  \mathtt{b}^kH_{n}^{(1-\alpha)}$ have a coincidence: there exists $k \le i \le  h_{n}-h_{n-1}$ such that $u_{[i,i+h_{n-1})}= w_{[i,i+h_{n-1} )}= H_{n-1}^{(0)}$.
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:Oprocha2D}
Let  $n \ge t, v >2$ be  integers and  $k= (k_{1}, k_{2}) \in \mathbb{Z}^2$  be a vector.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
	\item     If  $\|k\|_\infty = \max(|k_{1}|, |k_{2}|)  \le h_{n-1}/2$, then the blocks ${\bf H}_n^{(\alpha)}$ and  $T^{-k}{\bf H}_n^{(1-\alpha)}$, $\alpha= 0,1$, have a coincidence outside the ball of radius $\|k\|_\infty$. More precisely, there  is a position $i \in \mathbb{Z}^2$, $\|k\|_\infty \le \|i\|_\infty \le h_n-h_{n-1} $  such that
     ${\bf H}_n^{(\alpha)}|_{i+ [0,h_{n-1})^2} = {\bf H}_n^{(1-\alpha)}|_{i+k +[0,h_{n-1})^2} = {\bf H}_{n-1}^{(0)}$.

  \item If $h_{t-1}/2 <k_{1} \le  h_{t}/2$  and  $h_{v-1}/2 <k_{2} \le  h_{v}/2$, with $t,v \le n-2$ then the blocks ${\bf H}_{n}^{\alpha}$ and $T^{-k} {\bf H}_{n}^{\beta}$, $\alpha, \beta \in \{0,1\}$ have a coincidence. More precisely, denoting $u= \max (t, v)$, there is a position $q\in \mathbb{Z}^{2}$ such that $q+ [0, h_{u})^{2}$ and   $q+k+ [0, h_{u})^{2}$ are subset of $[0, h_{n})^{2}$ and 
  ${\bf H}_{n}^{(\alpha)}|_{q+ [0, h_{u})^{2}}=     {\bf H}_{n}^{(\beta)}|_{q+k+ [0, h_{u})^{2}} = {\bf H}_{{u}}^{(0)}$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Since the blocks are concatenations in the ${\bf e}_2$ direction of  blocks ${\bf H}_n^{(0, {\bf e}_1)}$ and ${\bf H}_n^{(1, {\bf e}_1)}\mathtt{a}^{{\bf e}_2}_{n-1}$ ordered as the words $H_n^{(0)}$, $H_n^{(1)}$, by Lemma \ref{lem:Oprocha} (i), there is an integer $i'_2 \ge |k_2|$ such that ${\bf H}_n^{(\alpha)}|_{(0,i'_2) + [0,h_{n}) \times [0,h_{n-1})} = {\bf H}_n^{(1-\alpha)}|_{(k_1,k_2+i'_2) + [0,h_{n}) \times [0,h_{n-1})} = {\bf H}_n^{(0, {\bf e}_1)}$. 
Notice that this level do not correspond to the top row of the block: precisely $i'_2 + k_2 +h_{n-1} < k_2 + h_n$, because the condition $\|k\|_\infty  \le h_{n-1}/2$ would impose  on the top rows of ${\bf H}_n^{(0)} $ and ${\bf H}_n^{(1)} $ to be equal. 


\noindent Since the block ${\bf H}_n^{(0, {\bf e}_1)}$ is always followed, in the ${\bf e}_2$ direction, by ${\bf H}_n^{(0, {\bf e}_1)}$ or  ${\bf H}_n^{(1, {\bf e}_1)}$,   
there is an integer  $i_2 \ge i'_2$ such that ${\bf H}_n^{(\alpha)}|_{(0,i_2) + [0,h_{n}) \times [0,h_{n-1})} ={\bf H}_n^{(\beta, {\bf e}_1)}$  and  \[{\bf H}_n^{(1-\alpha)}|_{(k_1,i_2+k_2) + [0,h_{n}) \times [0,h_{n-1})} = {\bf H}_n^{(1-\beta, {\bf e}_1)},\] for some $\beta \in \{0,1\}$.
Again,  Lemma \ref{lem:Oprocha} (i) applied to ${\bf H}_n^{(\beta, {\bf e}_1)}$ and $T^{-(k_{1},0)}{\bf H}_{n}^{(1-\beta, {\bf e}_{1})}$, gives the existence of an integer $i_1 \ge |k_1|$ such that $i =(i_1, i_2)$ satisfies the statement of Item (\textit{i}).


To prove Item (\textit{ii}), let us consider the case where  $v \ge t$. The other case is similar. The situation $\alpha\neq \beta$ is treated in item (\textit{i}), so we assume $\alpha=\beta$.
Recall that the left bottom block of ${\bf H}_{n}^{(\alpha)}$ is of the form ${\bf H}_{v+1}^{(0)}$. 
Since this block is always followed, in the ${\bf e}_1$ direction, by ${\bf H}_{v+1}^{(0)}$ or  ${\bf H}_{v+1}^{(1)}$,   
there is an integer  $i_2$ such that ${\bf H}_n^{(\alpha)}|_{(0,i_2h_{v+1}) + [0,h_{v+1})^{2}} ={\bf H}_{v+1}^{(1)}$  and  ${\bf H}_{n}^{(\alpha)}|_{(0,(i_{2}-1)h_{v+1}) + [0,h_{v+1})^{2}} = {\bf H}_{v+1}^{(0)}$.
By Item (\textit{i}), the blocks  $T^{-k} {\bf H}_{v+1}^{(0)}$ and ${\bf H}_{v+1}^{(1)}$ have a coincidence.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
The $\mathbb{Z}^2$-subshift $X= \overline{\textrm{orb}_T (x)}$ that is the orbit closure of $x$ is minimal and aperiodic, i.e the stabilizer of any point is trivial.
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
The block  $x|_{[-h_n, h_n)^2}$ appears in the left bottom of each block ${\bf H}_{n+1}^{(0)}$ which appears in each block  ${\bf H}_m^{(0)}$ and  ${\bf H}_m^{(1)}$, $m>n+1$. Hence $x|_{[-h_n, h_n)^2}$ occurs syndetically. The  orbit closure  $X$  for the shift map $T$ of $x$ is then a minimal subshift. 

The subshift being minimal, any potential period $v= (v_{1}, v_{2})\in \mathbb{Z}^2\setminus\{\bf 0\}$ is a period for $x$: $T^v x = x$.  Take $n$ large enough so that $h_{n-2}/2>\|v\|_\infty$. In particular this means that for each  integers $k\in \mathbb{Z}$, $m\in \mathbb{N}^{*}$, the patterns $T^{kv}\mathtt{a}_{m}^{{\bf e}_{1}}$ and $T^{kv}\mathtt{a}_{m}^{{\bf e}_{2}}$ occur in $x$. This imposes that there is a translated of a sublattice on which the restriction of $x$ is $\mathtt{a}$: there is a position $q \in \mathbb{Z}^{2}$ such that for any $(\ell^{1}, \ell^{2}) \in \mathbb{Z}^{2}$, $x_{q+(v_{1}\ell^{1}, v_{2} \ell^{2}) } = \mathtt{a}$.   So, this is also the case for the pattern ${\bf H}_{m}^{(0)}$. Since the patterns   ${\bf H}_{m}^{(0)}$ and $T^{-{\bf k}e_{i}} {\bf H}_{m}^{(0)}$, for  any  $m> n+2$, $ i= 1,2$ and $0 \le k \le \| v \|_{\infty}$, coincide on a pattern ${\bf H}_{n}^{(0)}$ (Lemma \ref{lem:Oprocha2D}), the pattern ${\bf H}_{n}^{(0)}$ is covered by all the translations of the lattice $(v_{1},0) \mathbb{Z} \oplus (0,v_{2})\mathbb{Z}$ where the coloration is constant to $\mathtt{a}$. Hence the coloration $x$ is constant, a contradiction.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
The system  $(X, T, \mathbb{Z}^{2})$ is doubly minimal.
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
To prove double minimality, it suffices to show that any pair of points $y,z$  in different orbits are proximal, i.e. the  distance   between $T^n y $  and $T^n z$ goes to zero for an infinite sequence of $n\in \mathbb{Z}^2$ \cite[p.747]{King_top_minimal_self_joinings:1990}.

Indeed, since $y$ is decomposed into blocks of the form
${\bf H}_n^{(0)} $ and ${\bf H}_n^{(1)}$,  
for each integer $n>0$, there are integers $ - h_n/2 \le i_1(n), i_2(n) \le h_n/2$  such that $y_{(i_1,i_2)+ [0, h_n)^2} = {\bf H}_n^{(\alpha_{n}, {\bf e}_2)}$, for some $\alpha_{n} \in \{0, 1\}$. Similarly, there are integers $ i_\ell - h_{n-2}/2 \le j_\ell(n) \le i_\ell+h_{n-2}/2$, $\ell= 1,2$ such that $z_{(j_1,j_2)+ [0, h_{n-2})^2} = {\bf H}_{n-2}^{(\beta_{n}, {\bf e}_2)}$  with  $\beta_{n} \in \{ 0,1\}$.  In particular, the indices $i_\ell$ and $j_\ell$ are close in the following sense $|i_\ell-j_\ell| \le h_{n-2}/2$. 
Let $v_n$ and $t_n$ be the smallest integers such that  $(h_{v_{n}-1}/2 <)$ $|i_2-j_2| \le h_{v_n}/2$ and  $(h_{t_{n}-1}/2 <)$ $|i_1-j_1| \le h_{t_n}/2$.

Setting $u_{n} = \max (v_{n}, t_{n})$, Lemma \ref{lem:Oprocha2D} (\textit{ii}) provides the existence of one  position $q_{n} \in \mathbb{Z}^{2}$ such that
\begin{align}\label{eq:Oprocha2}
y|_{q_{n}+ [0, h_{u_n})^{2} } = z|_{q_{n}+ [0, h_{u_n})^{2}} = {\bf H}_{u_{n}}^{(0)}.
\end{align}
In particular,  the elements $y$ and $z$ have coincidence.

Doing the former step for each integer $n$ provides integer sequences $(v_n)_{n\ge 0}$ and $(t_n)_{n \ge 0}$.  If  one of the two sequences is unbounded, the elements $y$ and $z$ coincide  on larger and larger set (of size the same size as ${\bf H}_{\max (v_n, t_n)}^{(0)}$ by equation \eqref{eq:Oprocha2}). Hence the two elements are proximal.

In contrast, let us consider the case where the two sequences $(v_n)_n$ and $(t_n)_n$ are bounded, Then, the positions $i(n) = (i_{1}, i_{2})$ and $j(n)= (j_{1}, j_{2})$ belong  to a bounded set. So, up to taking a subsequence of $n$, we can assume that the positions $i$ and $j$ are independent of $n$. 
We claim that the points $y$ and $z$ are proximal or either in the same orbit. To prove it, let us assume they are not proximal. This means they can not coincide on sufficiently large ball. So, there is some integer $N_1 >0$ such that $y$ and $z$ cannot coincide on any square of size $h_n$ for $n >N_1$. Lemma \ref{lem:Oprocha2D} (\textit{i}) then ensures that $y|_{i+[0, h_n)^2} = z|_{j+[0, h_n)^2} = {\bf H}_n^{(\alpha_n)}$ for the same $\alpha_n \in \{0,1\}$ for any $n>N_1+1$. Also, the same lemma gives that $y|_{i+ (\epsilon_1  h_n, \epsilon_2  h_n)+[0, h_n)^2} = z|_{j+(\epsilon_1  h_n, \epsilon_2  h_n)+[0, h_n)^2}$ for any $\epsilon_1, \epsilon_2 \in \{-1, 0, 1\}$. Since it is true for infinitely many $n$, this provides that $T^{-i}y= T^{-j}z$ meaning that $y$ and $z$ are in the same orbit.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
Let $(X,T,G)$ be a topological dynamical system that contains a $\varepsilon$-bi-asymptotic pair for every $\varepsilon>0$. Then $(X,T,G)$ is not strong doubly minimal.
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
Assume that $(X,T,G)$ is strong doubly minimal. As before, thanks to  \cref{lem:uncountable_aut,lem:coalescent}, take $\epsilon>0$ such that if $\phi \in \textnormal{Aut}(X,T,G)$ is such that $\|\phi\|\leq \epsilon$, then $\phi={\rm id}$. Let $(x,y)$ be a $\epsilon$-bi-asymptotic pair. It is clear that the orbit of $(x,y)$ is not $X\times X$, hence $y=T^g x$ for some $g\in Z(G)$. But this implies that $\|T^g\|\leq \epsilon$, and therefore $T^g={\rm id}$, contradicting that $x\neq y$.
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lem:diff_orbits}
    Let $(X,T,G)$ be a minimal topological dynamical system and assume that $(G,\rho)$ has thick horoballs. Suppose that for every $\varepsilon>0$ there exists $h\in\partial G$ with an $(h,\varepsilon)$-asymptotic pair. Then, for all $\varepsilon>0$ there exists $h\in\partial G$ and an $(h,\varepsilon)$-asymptotic pair $(x,y)$ such that $x$ and $y$ lie on different $Z(G)$ orbits.
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
Suppose there exists $\varepsilon_0>0$ such that $\|T^g\|> \varepsilon_0$ for all $g\in Z(G)\setminus\{{\rm Id}\}$. Let $0<\varepsilon\leq \varepsilon_0$. Consider $h\in\partial G$, and an $(h,\varepsilon)$-asymptotic pair $(x,y)$ with $x\neq y$. Suppose there exists $g_0\in Z(G)$ such that $y = T^{g_0}x$. Then, $d(T^gx,T^{g_0g}x)\leq \varepsilon$ for all $g\in\{h<0\}$. Because $\{h<0\}$ is thick, and the action is minimal, by~\cref{lem:minimal_por_horobola} $\{T^gx \mid g\in\{h<0\}\}$ is dense. By continuity, $\|T^{g_0}\|\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_0$. This implies $g_0 = 1_G$, which in turn implies $x = y$. Therefore, $x$ and $y$ lie in different $Z(G)$ orbits.

So, we may assume that there exists $g_n \in Z(G)$ with $\|T^{g_n}\|\to0$. By \cref{lem:uncountable_aut}, there exists $\phi\notin\{T^g \mid g\in Z(G)\}$ with $\sup_{x\in X} d(\phi(x),x) \leq \varepsilon$. In particular, for any $x \in X$ we have $\phi(x)\notin Z(G)x$. Then $(x,\phi(x))$ are $\varepsilon$-asymptotic (for any $h$).
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- theorem-12: body_md -->
\label{thm:king_general}
Let $(X,T,G)$ be a topological dynamical system. Assume that there exists $N\in \mathbb{N}$ such that for every $\varepsilon>0$ there exist points $x_i,y_i\in X$ for $i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}$ and sets $\Omega_i^{(\varepsilon)}\subseteq G$ such that 
\begin{itemize}
    \item The points $x_i$ and $y_i$ are distinct, and $d(T^gx_i,T^gy_i)\leq \varepsilon$ for all $g\in \Omega_i^{(\varepsilon)}$.
    \item The collection $\Omega_1^{(\varepsilon)},\ldots,\Omega_N^{(\varepsilon)}$ is thickly full.
\end{itemize}

Then $(X,T,G)$ does not have $2N$-fold sTMSJ.
<!-- end theorem-12 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
Note that the assumptions imply that $X$ is infinite. Assume that $(X,T,G)$ does have $2N$-fold sTMSJ. Let $\varepsilon_0>0$ be such that $\|T^g\|> \varepsilon_0$ for all $g\in Z(G)\setminus\{1_G\}$. For $0<\varepsilon\leq \varepsilon_0$, let $(x_i,y_i)$, and $\Omega_i$, $i=1,\ldots,N$ given as in the statement of \cref{thm:king_general}. Then we must have that $x_i$ and $y_i$ lie in different $Z(G)$ orbits. Indeed, if $y_i=T^{g}x_i$ for some $g\in Z(G)$, by minimality of $(X,T,G)$ and thickness of $\Omega_i$, we would get $\|T^g\|\leq \varepsilon$, and hence $T^g={\rm id}$, contradicting that $x_i\neq y_i$. 
Let $A=\{z_1,\ldots,z_k\}$ be a maximal subset of $\{x_1,y_1,\ldots,x_N,y_N\}$ with the property that all of its elements lie in different $Z(G)$-orbits. Since $x_i,y_i$ do not lie in the same $Z(G)$-orbit,  we have $2\leq k \leq 2N$. 
For $i=1,\ldots,N$, set $h_i,k_i\in Z(G)$ such that $T^{h_i}x_i, T^{k_i}y_i\in A$ (these exist by maximality of $A$) and write $T^{h_i}x_i=z_{a(i)}$, $T^{k_i}y_i=z_{b(i)}$, for $a(i),b(i)\in \{1,\ldots,k\}$. Note that for all $1\leq i\leq N$, $a(i)\neq b(i)$ since $x_i$ and $y_i$ lie in different $Z(G)$-orbits.  
Consider the tuple $(z_1,\ldots,z_k)$. By construction, all coordinates of this tuple lie in different $Z(G)$-orbits, and since $(X,T,G)$ has $2N$-fold sTMSJ (and in particular has $k$-fold sTMSJ), the orbit of $(z_1,\ldots,z_k)$ is dense in $X^k$. Let $(w_1,\ldots,w_k) \in X^k$, and let $(g_j)_{j\in \mathbb{N}}$ be a sequence in $G$ such that $T^{g_j}z_i\to w_i$ as $j\to \infty$, for all $i=1,\ldots,k$. Taking a subsequence if needed, we may assume that $g_j\in \Omega_t$ for some $t\in \{1,\ldots,N\}$. This implies that  $d(T^{g_j}x_t,T^{g_j}y_t)\leq \varepsilon$ for all $j$ and $(T^{g_j}T^{h_t}x_t,T^{g_j}T^{k_t}y_t)=(T^{g_j}z_{a(t)}, T^{g_j}z_{b(t)})\to (w_{a(t)},w_{b(t)})$.  We conclude that $w_{b(t)} \in T^{k_t}B(T^{h_t^{-1}}w_{a(t)},\varepsilon)$. Recalling that $a(t)\neq b(t)$ we obtain:
 \begin{equation}
  \tag{P}\label{eq:property_asympt}
  \parbox{\dimexpr\linewidth-7em}{
  For any $(w_1,\ldots,w_k) \in X^k$, there exist $1\leq a<b\leq k$ such that \[w_b\in \bigcup_{1\leq t\leq N} T^{k_t}B(T^{h_t^{-1}}w_a,\varepsilon) \cup T^{h_t}B(T^{k_t^{-1}}w_{a},\varepsilon)\]
  }
\end{equation}

\begin{claim}
Fix $L \in \mathbb{N}$. For any small enough $\varepsilon>0$, any finite set of points $w_1,\ldots,w_L \in X$ and $h_1,\ldots,h_{L}\in Z(G)$, the set 
$\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq L} T^{h_i^{-1}}B(w_i,\varepsilon)$ is not equal to $X$. 
\end{claim}
\begin{proof}[Proof of the claim]
Assume for the sake of contradiction that the conclusion is not true. Take $\varepsilon_n\to 0$,  $w_{1,n},\ldots,w_{L,n} \in X$, $h_{1,n},\ldots,h_{L,n}\in Z(G)$ such that 
\[ X=\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq L} T^{h_{i,n}^{-1}}B(w_{i,n},\varepsilon_n)\]
Note that this implies that $Z(G)$ is infinite, otherwise the equicontinuity of the family $\{T^{h}\}_{h\in Z(G)}$ would imply that $X$ is finite.
As $Z(G)$ is infinite, choose $h^{(1)},...,h^{(L+1)}\in Z(G)$ such that the corresponding maps $T^{h^{(1)}},...,T^{h^{(L+1)}}$ are distinct. Fix $x\in X$. Then, for every $n\in\mathbb{N}$ by the pigeonhole principle, there exists $q\neq p$ and $i_n$ such that $T^{h^{(p)}}x$ and $T^{h^{(q)}}x$ belong to $T^{h_{i_n,n}^{-1}}B(w_{i_n,n},\varepsilon_n)$. Up to a subsequence, we suppose $p$ and $q$ do not depend on $n$. Then, if we denote $h = h^{(p)}$ and $h'=h^{(q)}$, we have that $h, h'\in Z(G)$. Then, $d(T^{h}T^{h_{i,n}}x,T^{h'}T^{h_{i,n}}x) \leq 2\varepsilon_n$. Again, taking a subsequence if needed, we may assume $T^{h_{i_n,n}}x\to x'$ for some $x'\in X$, and so $T^{h}x'=T^{h'}x'$. By~\cref{lem:min+faithfull=>free}, this implies $T^{h}=T^{h'}$, which contradicts the choice of elements $h^{(j)}$.
\end{proof}

Given $\varepsilon>0$ so that the claim above holds for $L=2kN$, we can construct a point $(w_1,\ldots,w_k)\in X^k$ for which $\eqref{eq:property_asympt}$ does not hold. Indeed, start with any $w_1$ and choose $w_2$ so that $\eqref{eq:property_asympt}$ does not hold for $w_b=w_2$ and $w_a=w_1$. Such $w_2$ exists thanks to the previous claim. Inductively, if we have chosen $w_1,\ldots,w_l$, we pick $w_{l+1}$ such that it is not in $ \bigcup_{i\leq l}\bigcup_{1\leq t\leq N} T^{k_t}B(T^{h_t^{-1}}w_i,\varepsilon) \cup T^{h_t}B(T^{k_t^{-1}}w_{i},\varepsilon)$. Thanks to the claim, such a $w_{l+1}$ always exists. Finishing with $l+1=k$, we find the announced point. Hence $(X,T,G)$ does not have $k$-fold (and hence $2N$-fold) sTMSJ.
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- claim-2: body_md -->
Fix $L \in \mathbb{N}$. For any small enough $\varepsilon>0$, any finite set of points $w_1,\ldots,w_L \in X$ and $h_1,\ldots,h_{L}\in Z(G)$, the set 
$\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq L} T^{h_i^{-1}}B(w_i,\varepsilon)$ is not equal to $X$.
<!-- end claim-2 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
Assume for the sake of contradiction that the conclusion is not true. Take $\varepsilon_n\to 0$,  $w_{1,n},\ldots,w_{L,n} \in X$, $h_{1,n},\ldots,h_{L,n}\in Z(G)$ such that 
\[ X=\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq L} T^{h_{i,n}^{-1}}B(w_{i,n},\varepsilon_n)\]
Note that this implies that $Z(G)$ is infinite, otherwise the equicontinuity of the family $\{T^{h}\}_{h\in Z(G)}$ would imply that $X$ is finite.
As $Z(G)$ is infinite, choose $h^{(1)},...,h^{(L+1)}\in Z(G)$ such that the corresponding maps $T^{h^{(1)}},...,T^{h^{(L+1)}}$ are distinct. Fix $x\in X$. Then, for every $n\in\mathbb{N}$ by the pigeonhole principle, there exists $q\neq p$ and $i_n$ such that $T^{h^{(p)}}x$ and $T^{h^{(q)}}x$ belong to $T^{h_{i_n,n}^{-1}}B(w_{i_n,n},\varepsilon_n)$. Up to a subsequence, we suppose $p$ and $q$ do not depend on $n$. Then, if we denote $h = h^{(p)}$ and $h'=h^{(q)}$, we have that $h, h'\in Z(G)$. Then, $d(T^{h}T^{h_{i,n}}x,T^{h'}T^{h_{i,n}}x) \leq 2\varepsilon_n$. Again, taking a subsequence if needed, we may assume $T^{h_{i_n,n}}x\to x'$ for some $x'\in X$, and so $T^{h}x'=T^{h'}x'$. By~\cref{lem:min+faithfull=>free}, this implies $T^{h}=T^{h'}$, which contradicts the choice of elements $h^{(j)}$.
<!-- end proof-40 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
Let $(X,T,G)$ be a topological dynamical system, where $G$ is endowed with a right-invariant and proper metric. Assume that there exist a finite set $K\subseteq G$ and $N\in \mathbb{N}$ such that for any $\varepsilon>0$, there exist $h_1,\ldots,h_N\in \textnormal{ND}_{\varepsilon}(X)$, with $\{h_i<0\}$ thick, and $g_{i,k}\in Z(G)$, for $1\leq i\leq N$, $k\in K$, such that 
\[\bigcup_{k\in K} \bigcup_{1\leq i\leq N} kg_{i,k}\{h_i<0\} =G\]
Then, $(X,T,G)$ has no $2|K|N$ sTMSJ.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-41: body_md -->
Consider $\varepsilon>0$. Because $K$ is finite, there exists $0<\delta\leq \varepsilon$ such that $d(x,y)\leq \delta$ implies $d(T^kx,T^ky)\leq \varepsilon$ for all $k\in K$. By applying the hypothesis to $\delta$, there exists $h_1,\ldots,h_N\in \textnormal{ND}_{\delta}(X)$, with $\{h_i<0\}$ thick, and $g_{i,k}\in Z(G)$, $1\leq i\leq N$, $k\in K$ such that 
\[\bigcup_{k\in K} \bigcup_{1\leq i\leq N} kg_{i,k}\{h_i<0\} =G.\]

Since $h_i\in\textnormal{ND}_{\delta}(X)$ for every $i$, there exist $x_i\neq y_i$ in $X$ with $d(T^gx_i,T^gy_i)\leq \delta$ for all $g\in\{h_i<0\}$. For each pair $(i,k)$ we define $\Omega_{i,k} = k g_{i,k}\{h_i<0\}$, and the set of distinct points $x_{i,k} =T^{g_{i,k}^{-1}}x_i$ and $y_{i,k} = T^{g_{i,k}^{-1}}y_i$. Take $f = kg_{i,k}g\in\Omega_{i,k}$. Because $g$ belongs to the center $f g_{i,k}^{-1} = kg$, and therefore $T^f x_{i,k} = T^{kg} x_i$ and $T^f y_{i,k} = T^{kg} y_i$. Thus, $d(T^fx_{i,k}, T^fy_{i,k})\leq\varepsilon$.

The $|K|N$ sets $\Omega_{i,k}$ cover $G$. Since the elements $g_{i,k}$ belong to the center, we have $kg_{i,k}\{h_i<0\} = k\{h_i<0\}g_{i,k}$. Because right translation preserves thickness, it suffices to see that $k\{h_i<0\}$ is thick. Given $r > 0$, the ball $B_{r}(1_G)$ is finite, so $R = \max\{\rho(k^{-1}f'k,1_G) \mid f'\in B_r(1_G)\}$ is finite. Consider $g$ such that $B_R(g)\subseteq \{h_i<0\}$. If $\rho(f,kg) \le r$, then $f' = f(kg)^{-1}$ satisfies $f'\in B_r(1_G)$, and $k^{-1}fg^{-1} = k^{-1}f'k$. Then, $\rho(k^{-1}f, g) \le R$ and $f \in k\{h_i<0\}$. Thus $B_{r}(kg)\subseteq k\{h_i<0\}$ for every $r$. This implies the sets and points satisfy the hypothesis of~\Cref{thm:king_general}, and thus $X$ has no $2|K|N$-fold sTMSJ.
<!-- end proof-41 -->
<!-- proof-42: body_md -->
First, assume that the torsion subgroup of $G$, $Tor(G)$, is trivial, so that $G \simeq \mathbb{Z}^d$. 
    By Proposition~\ref{prop:descomp_Z} there exists $\{v_i\}_{i=1}^k\subseteq\textnormal{ND}(X)$ with $k\leq d+1$ such that
    \[\bigcup_{i=1}^k\{g\in\mathbb{Z}^d \mid \langle v_i, g\rangle\leq 0\} = \mathbb{Z}^d.\]
    Denote $\Omega_i=\{g\in \mathbb{Z}^d \mid \langle v_i, g\rangle \leq 0\}$ and $\Omega_i'=\{g\in \mathbb{Z}^d \mid \langle v_i, g\rangle < 0\}$. For each $i$, we can find $g_i\in \mathbb{Z}^d$ such that $\Omega_i+g_i\subseteq \Omega_i'$. Note that for $x,y\in X$ such that $d(T^gx,T^gy)\leq \varepsilon$ for all $g\in \Omega_i'$, we have that $d(T^{g}(T^{g_i}x),T^{g}(T^{g_i}y))\leq \varepsilon$ for all $g\in \Omega_i$. 
    
    Noting that the collection $\Omega_1,\ldots,\Omega_k$ is thickly full, we get that it fulfills the conditions of \cref{thm:king_general}, and we obtain the desired conclusion. 

   For the general case, as the action of $Tor(G)$ is equicontinuous, it is not hard to check that, for the $v_i$ as before, $\tilde{\Omega}_i=\Omega_i\oplus Tor(G)$  satisfies the hypothesis of \cref{thm:king_general}. The conclusion follows.
<!-- end proof-42 -->
<!-- proof-43: body_md -->
By Proposition~\ref{prop:descomp_H} there exists $\{(\vec{a}_i,\vec{b}_i,c_i)\}_{i=1}^k\subseteq\textnormal{ND}(X)$ with $k\leq 2d+1$ such that
    \[\bigcup_{i=1}^k\left\{(\vec{v},\vec{u},t)\in H_{2d+1}(\mathbb{Z}) \mid \langle V(\vec{a}_i,\vec{b}_i,c_i), (\vec{v},\vec{u})\rangle\leq 0\right\} = H_{2d+1}(\mathbb{Z}).\]
    The conclusion follows from \cref{thm:king_general}, using the same arguments as in the proof of \cref{thm:no-tmjs-Zd}.
<!-- end proof-43 -->
